Genealogy Proofs, by John Mark Browder

Providing accurate information on the Browder and related families

The Browder Family Surname

By: John Mark Browder, B.A., M.S., M.T.S., M.Div.

Having recently found more documents concerning the Browder family in England and Ireland, I can definitively state the following based on said documents.

  1. As I have stated before, the Browder surname originated in England. It is NOT an anglicized form or variant of some other surname.
  2. Having stated this, our surname is an abbreviation or contraction of the word “Browderer”, which was being used as a surname as early as the late 1300s, according to the records.
  3. Also, from the records it becomes obvious that the letter “w” was dropped first. Therefore, we have the surnames “Browderer” and “Broderer” being used early on. Later, both became contracted so that the “er” was dropped from each, producing the surnames “Browder” and “Broder”. There is no reason to posit anything more complicated than that Broder was formed from Broderer and Browder was formed from Browderer. The only exceptions are misspellings in some records, resulting in variations such as “Broader” and “Broadar”.
  4. Again, our surname has absolutely no connection whatsoever with surnames such as Broderick. And the Broderick family Coat of Arms does not belong to the Browder family.
  5. In addition, there never has been, as far as I can tell from any records, any variant form such as “O’Browder” or “O’Broder” or anything of the like. I have not seen a single record that places an “O” in front of either of these surnames or any variant spelling. Not one.
  6. Members of our family, regardless of surname spelling, resided in various parts of England from early on. They appear to have first resided in London and spread out from there.
  7. Incidentally, among the favorite early Browder given male names appear to have been William and Thomas, followed by John. After a while, Robert became common, it seems. Mary and Katherine appear to have been the early favorites for females.
  8. It appears to me that members of this family spread into northern counties last. Therefore, they arrived in Yorkshire and Lanchestershire later than other places, but not that much later. Still, I have yet to find a single record placing any family members in Wales or Scotland early on. That doesn’t mean they weren’t there, but no record has yet been found to show whether they were or not.
  9. Even to this date, the earliest indication that the Browder family was in Ireland comes from the 1664 record cited in my books. In that record, the name is spelled “Browder”, period. In all other Irish records that I have seen, the name is spelled either as “Browder” or “Broder”, with only one exception. This shows that the “er” ending was likely dropped before any family members, other than that one, moved to Ireland, which makes perfect sense if they came from Lanchestershire directly to Ireland as I have previously posited.
  10. Even though I have not found a record of our Edmund Browder, who likely emigrated to the American Colonies as an infant or young person, in any Irish or English records, the fact that I did find one Edmunder, son of Edmunder Broderer and Ellena Walsh, from a March 1854 Catholic baptismal record, is telling in more than one way. (1) It again demonstrates that the “w” was dropped, retaining the “er” ending. (2) This record all by itself shows no linkage whatsoever to surnames such as Broderick. (3) Since this is a record showing the baptism into the Roman Catholic Church, I suggest that the very dropping of the ending “er” indicated the transition in England from Roman Catholicism to Protestantism. Thus, whoever moved from England to Ireland in this particular instance came from a lineage that had retained its membership in the Roman Catholic Church without fail. This would signify that the line we early American Browders came from was probably already Protestant by the time they crossed over into Ireland, for they would have been unlikely to convert after moving to heavily Roman Catholic Ireland.
  11. This is why, in any present or future research, those attempting to trace the family should consider all of the above information when trying to establish relationships. In so many words, Browders will be Browders and Broders will be Broders – all of these Protestant with their forebears, Browderers and Broderers having been Catholic. That should help the researcher to sort people out when the records are unclear. So, there won’t be a Broder in one generation and a Browder in the next, for example. Once the “w” was dropped, it was final. Same with the “er” ending. No generation will have gone from Browder to Browderer, for example. And no generation will proceed from “Broder” to “Browder” in the next, adding the “w” back into their surname. It just isn’t going to happen that way.
  12. All of this makes it even more likely that we Browders who are descended from Edmund and Elizabeth are also descended from Robert Browder and Mary Hulme, as I have posited for years. That because it was no big leap from Manchester to Ireland.
  13. Let me add that, in some records, only an “e” is added to the end of a surname or even a given name. That goes for all names, not just ours. That’s just the way they wrote back then, and it is of no consequence when figuring out family relationships or anything else. So, sometimes one will even see the surname spelled “Browderere”. It happened.
  14. Finally, an even later record of one Edmund Broderer from an Albany, New York record of 1891 and 1892 is likely of the afore-mentioned Edmunder Broderer of Ireland. Thus, he would have immigrated to New York, taking just about the same route as our earlier ancestors did except that they went to Virginia instead. It is interesting to note that he was a boot and shoe maker, which falls in line with our ancestor, Edmund Browder, who, in addition to having been a tobacco merchant, also sold shoes. If he sold shoes, he likely made them too. There is literally no distance between earning one’s living as an embroiderer and clothing maker and earning one’s living as a shoe maker. However distant the family relationship may have been, the family trade carried on.

On Surnames and Coats of Arms

It seems to me, because I am still being asked certain questions that I thought I had clarified for everyone, that it is time for what I hope will be a good-natured rant. See, there are times when you just realize that you still haven’t got your point across on something and you have to figure out another way to try and do that. So, here it is.

As in most cases where genealogy and family history are concerned, I concentrate on my own family name. That is not only the case because I am a Browder, but it is also the case because it is from that family line that most people’s questions still come from. I apologize for the fact that this is sometimes frustrating for me, and I sometimes let that be known a bit. The fact of the matter is that, with most questions, the information is, in fact, out there, and is easily found. The problem, however, is that there is also still a lot of misinformation out there that causes people to still have questions. I truly wish all of the misinformation could be eliminated, and I do think this will indeed happen over time. But, in the mean-time, I have also seen some of this misinformation get spread on brand-new posts, all because the person who made the post did not do due diligence when doing, um, “research”. It is literally like trying to stop a virus. I think we all have some idea how difficult that is now, due to recent events. Once it is out there, it is nearly impossible to stop or even contain. Hence, the meaning of “going viral”.

But, please don’t think that this is a recent phenomenon that has been happening only since the invention of the internet. No, it has been happening for a long time. An author would write something in a book, admittedly doing the best that he/she could, speculating about something, and on down the line people started taking it as fact. It’s very much like an old Pentecostal revival or tent meeting, during which, in one particular instance, a certain woman read one passage from the Bible that said people should be baptized in the name of Jesus, and from that she and others extrapolated that this was the only formula that people should follow, and created a whole denomination based on that. Never mind that other passages provided a variant formula of baptism in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost – for these people, that simply MEANS the name of Jesus. And you can’t talk them out of it, because they have completely accepted this without reservation.

The same sort of thing has happened with reference to the Browder surname. Some author many years ago speculated, based on the evidence she had at the time (which isn’t everything we have today) that the Browder surname might have originated in Ireland and might be related to the Irish family name “Broderick”. But, because of this speculation, other people, later, who read her book, took that as fact and have run with it ever since. And you can’t hardly dissuade any of these people from the idea that the Browder family is indigenously Irish, no matter what sort of evidence you present. It has practically become a point of religious belief for them. It simply does not matter to them whether you find evidence to the contrary or not, since they have always believed this, they will continue to do so. And, after all, they themselves can find evidence for their point on the internet!

This is just a small picture, demonstrating what I have been up against in trying to correct things over the years. It is literally, probably, going to take a couple of generations for actual facts to be accepted by most people. But, let me make the effort once again in a way that I have not before.

Let us begin with the concept and development of surnames. Now, I have found my studies in history, generally, to have been of great help when doing genealogical research, and vice-versa. I, personally, think that anyone should have a good grasp not only of history in general, but also of family history, so that they can see how history unfolded and understand at least a little bit of how their own ancestors played a part. In that light, the combination of general history with family history becomes important if one is to truly understand how things unfolded.

Surnames are actually NOT a recent development in history. People had surnames way back in ancient times. Not every culture did, but some did, most notably the Romans. For the Roman, their family name, or surname, was of utmost importance to them. It literally tied them to their family, and to their inheritance. A Roman of the upper classes, especially, would likely have as many as 3, 4, or 5 actual names. So, he might have a name like Pious Opus Castellus Paaetextatus Magnus, or whatever. His family name, or surname, was Praetextatus. His given name was Pious (that is what the couple would have named him when he was born – “What should we call him? Let’s call him Pious”). All the other names followed from family names on his father’s and mother’s sides of the family automatically, with the exception of Magnus, which he would have earned for doing something great. So, with the Roman of noble birth, at least, if you knew all of his names, you automatically knew EXACTLY who he was. You already knew who his family was and everything. There could be no mistake.

Again, that was not the case with most other societies. Most people back in ancient, and on into medieval, times were lucky if they had one single name. Alfred, or whatever. Some children never were actually named, but came to be called something, which became their name. When I was a kid, I was often called “big ears” because I heard literally EVERYTHING. Back in ancient or early medieval times, that might have become my actual name, because there was nothing else to designate me by. Or, I might have made a name for myself by the things that I did. If I did something great, like conquer a certain city, I might begin to be called by that city’s name, with the reminder of “otherwise known as ‘big ears’”. That way, I would have still been identified as the guy who used to be called “big ears”, but was now known as Antinopolus, because that was the city I had conquered.

One knows that surnames had not developed, at least not very much, in ancient times in most cultures, because practically everyone in the Bible is called by only one name. Most of them literally only had that one name. They were often differentiated by the addition of a designation as to where they were from. Joseph of Aramathea is one of these. There were probably a thousand Josephs who were from that city at the same time as this guy lived, by the way, but he is the only one who made it into the Bible. Thus, he is forever known in this manner. As for the Romans mentioned in the New Testament, they are given only one name, not because they didn’t have more than one name (because we know they had several names), but because the biblical writer either didn’t know their full names, or didn’t care. But, trust me, Governor Felix had more than just the name “Felix”.

Anyway, once the Roman Empire fell, surnames generally came into disuse in Europe and surrounding areas. The barbarians who helped to cause the fall generally didn’t have surnames, and the ones who did generally only had that, plus their given name – thus two names rather than 3, 4, or 5. Some people continued to carry on their surnames, especially in Italy, but this was still a rarity, mainly among the nobility. Everybody else, again, was reduced to being lucky if they had one given name. So, Francus was exactly that – Francus. That’s all.

Slowly, over time, surnames began to develop once again during late medieval times. Most surnames developed from a person’s trade, so they were trade names. For example – Smith. This name came from the fact that the person who adopted it for himself, or came to be called by it, was, by trade, a smith. He forged items from iron and other metals. That’s what he did for a living, so he came to be designated by that term. The trouble with the surname Smith is that, since people worked as smiths all over the world, not to mention Europe itself, the term “smith” came to be used as a surname by a wide variety of people, many of whom were in no way whatsoever related to each other. John Smythe who lived in London, England was in no way related to Yohann Smithus who lived in Rome, Italy. But, over time, many, many families who adopted this term for their surname, regardless of variants in spelling based on language and culture, began to spell their name simply as “Smith”, because everybody else was doing this and it was just easier to fit in that way. So, if your surname is Smith, you may have a rather hard time tracing your family tree, because (1) there are so many Smiths in the world and (2) you don’t know, without a LOT of research just where your name should start being spelled “Smythe” or something else.

What that means, in a nutshell, is that we Browders are VERY lucky with our name. Why? Well, let me explain, first, that the Browder surname also comes from a trade, like the Smith surname does. One would think, then, that the same sorts of problems would exist for us too. But, they don’t. The simple reason for this is because the Browder surname was not adopted by all that many people at its beginning. We can see this by the progression of the name, which I drew out in both of my books. In so many words, we don’t have someone in Tuscany, Italy adopting the Browder surname, while at the same time having someone in Glasgow, Scotland adopting the same name because they had the same trade. It just didn’t happen that way for us.

But, might some unrelated families in different areas of Europe have adopted SIMILAR names, based on the same trade? YES. And, just as in the case of “Smith”, they would have been spelled differently. But, the fact of the matter is that I have found no actual evidence that this did, in fact, take place. It COULD have, but there is no evidence for it. There is ample evidence concerning the various spellings of “Smith” in different areas of Europe, but there isn’t for “Browder”.

What the meagre evidence that we do have demonstrates is that the Browder surname popped up in one place originally, and one place ONLY. And it wasn’t Ireland. We KNOW this because we have the documentation to prove it now, whereas, in earlier times, this documentation had not been found by others. So, they simply made mistakes because they did not have enough documentation. One cannot fault them for that because they did the best they could with what they had, just as I also do. BUT, we can fault those who continue to perpetuate the incorrect information when the correct information is out there and so easily accessible – because I have put it out there all over the internet. I KNOW it’s out there for the taking.

Anyway, getting back to our surname – The records that we do have demonstrate that Flemish weavers/embroiderers emigrated to England to ply their trade at the King’s invitation. There were lots of them. Many of them would have been related because relatives generally remained in the same trade. That’s just how it was back in medieval times. Eventually, some of these people began to be called by their given name, as well as being designated by their trade. This would ONLY have taken place with reference to the patriarch of the family, however, because he would have been the only one who generally interacted with the rest of society. The rest of the family simply worked and generally remained home. That’s just how it was back in medieval times. And every member of the family, generally, had some part in the same trade as the patriarch did. So, they were all in it together as a family unit.

Thus, the patriarch, who would have become fairly well-known in the local community, would have been known as, for example, William the Browderer, because “browdering” was his (and his family’s) trade. He was a “browderer”. His family members were browderers too, but they would not have been known in the wider community and would not necessarily have been designated as such if they even left home to, say, go shopping. Nobody would really have known them unless they were introduced. But, they would have been around, or with, the patriarch, so other people would have been able to know that they were of the same family.

During this time-period, inheritance was almost always a one-way street. The eldest son was automatically going to inherit practically everything – everything of any importance. That included the family name. So, the patriarch of the family, from the birth of this son, would have been grooming him to take over the family business. As he came to maturity and got married and began a family, he also would have been designated by his trade name just as his father had been. He would have, gradually, become known in the wider community also so that, when the patriarch died or could no longer do his job, this son would have been ready to take over. Over a few generations, dropping “the” from the patriarch’s designation took place, so that the grandson of William the Browderer might have been Hugh Browderer. This is how it happened.

Now, let me be clear; every son of the patriarch of any given family would have tended to continue in the same trade. But only the eldest would have inherited much of anything. Thus, the other sons would have had to, more or less, strike out on their own while continuing their associations with the rest of the family, all of them more or less working together, and sometimes also competing with each other. It would have been an interesting dance to see.

Sometimes younger sons would tend to move away to a different location so that they could become more independent. That way, they became patriarchs in their own right; still continuing the same business into which they were brought up. More than likely, this is exactly why we see the surname “Browderer” being shortened, at some point, to simply “Browder”. It was yet another way for a younger son to differentiate himself from the rest of the family, while still plying the same trade elsewhere.

Now, where exactly do we see this take place? In England, and NOWHERE ELSE. Family members from Flanders first settled in and around London where they started their trade and began to be referred to by the surname “Browderer”. Sometimes the name was also spelled “Browderere”, probably by mistake. You know, spelling was not all that important back in those days and few could do a really good job of it. So, mistakes were common. But, what we DON’T see in London and surrounding areas is the shortened version “Browder”. We only see that, for the very first time, in a marriage record from 1623 in Manchester, England.

Again, this is something that earlier researchers simply did not know about. If they had, it seems to me that all speculation about the “Irishness” of the Browder surname would never have taken place to begin with. That is not to say that people from Ireland did not emigrate to England, and vice-versa, earlier than this date. That is not at all what I am saying. What I AM saying is that there is absolutely no reason to posit that anyone with the Browder surname would have emigrated from Ireland to Manchester, England because there is no reason to correlate the Browder surname with Ireland at all that early. The actual evidence demonstrates a progression from those, in London, England, who went by the surname “Browderer”, to those in Manchester, England, who went by the surname “Browder”. You really can’t get away from that. There is absolutely no reason to posit anything else at all.

So, later, in 1664, when we find a record of a Thomas Browder who lived in Kilkenny, next to County Tipperary, Ireland, the logical progression is that members of the family from Manchester, England settled in Kilkenny, Ireland later and started their own patriarchal trade family unit there, just as members from London, England settled in Manchester, England earlier to do the same thing. Again, there is literally no reason to posit any other scenario, based on the evidence that we have. This latter family was just another later generation wishing to differentiate itself from those in Manchester, so they moved to a different location in order to do that. That’s just how it was done back then. That some of them, in order to further differentiate themselves, might have shortened their surname to “Broder” is entirely possible, and even logical. BUT, none of them had any connection whatsoever to the family Broderick, which WAS indigenously Irish. There is absolutely no reason to posit that they did. It simply does not fit.

Now, I am truly sorry that the internet is such a cesspool of misinformation, and doubly-sorry that people perpetuate misinformation there on a grand scale. People, as I see it, would much rather perpetuate a comfortable falsehood than an uncomfortable truth. That’s just human nature. People do not like to let go of cherished ideas and beliefs, especially if said ideas and beliefs have been held for a long time. So, please believe me, if there was ANY real connection with the Broderick family that could be demonstrated by actual evidence, I would gladly bring it out here and now. But, there isn’t. And that includes the fanciful idea, perpetuated all over the internet, that the Browder family can in any way lay claim to the Coat of Arms of the Broderick family. It simply is NOT ours to lay claim to, period.

When I was growing up, my grandparents had a Coat of Arms on their wall, which was supposed to have been for the Browder family. Eventually, after I asked about it, they found and let me make a copy of the certificate that had come with it. I still have a copy of that certificate. It explains that this particular Coat of Arms came from Rietstap Armorial General and also explains what the symbolism on said Coat of Arms means. It also explains that the original family member who bore this Coat of Arms is unknown. So, it is unknown who this Coat of Arms was originally designed for. But, it is known that he was a member of our family.

I should not need to even say that this Coat of Arms has no resemblance whatsoever to that of the Broderick family. It is described thus: “Une pique d’arg., ensanglantee de gu. D’arg.: au chef de sin., ch. de deux fers de pique de champ, ensanglantes de gu. Cq. Cour”, meaning, “Silver, upper third green with two heads of a pike stained with red blood”. It is also to include a crest, which description reads: “Une pique d’arg., ensanglantes de gu.”, meaning, “A silver pike head stained with red blood”. No motto is recorded with this Coat of Arms, indicating that no battle cry was registered with it. It is fairly safe to say, then, that this Coat of Arms had nothing to do with battle. What the symbolism actually means is not quite certain, but violence of some kind seems to be indicated. And, it is known that foreigners, such as Flemings, did endure persecution and violence in England at times simply because they were foreigners and Roman Catholics. This Coat of Arms, by the way, can in fact, be found on the internet, but a truly diligent search has to be made in order to do so because most of the Coats of Arms that are shown are of the Broderick family, with our name on them. I have, in fact, reached out to some of these entities, trying to get them to correct this, but they simply will not do it.

Finally, the fact that the Browder surname, correctly spelled as we do today, is found in that one instance in Ireland DOES lend credence to the idea that the family immigrated FROM Ireland to the American Colonies, but does not even suggest that the Browder surname is indigenously Irish or even related to that of the Broderick family. So, YES, our family most likely did sail from Ireland to the American Colonies. THAT is our connection to Ireland – living in Ireland for, perhaps, one or two generations, and then moving on to the Colonies. It really doesn’t have to be more complicated than that. They set out once more to differentiate themselves from those they left behind, but this time didn’t change the spelling of the name. Members who had presumably changed their name’s spelling to “Broder” did the same thing separately. It’s that simple.

Captain Isham Browder

Captain Isham Browder

By: John Mark Browder, B.A., M.S., M.T.S., M.Div. (updated and corrected Jan. 8, 2025)

Isham Browder (c1732-1784) of Guilford County, North Carolina was selected as a delegate to the Fifth Revolutionary Provincial Congress of North Carolina.

“At a congress begun and held at Halifax, in the county of Halifax, the twelfth day of November, in the year of our Lord one thousand seven hundred and seventy-six. It being certified that the following persons were duly elected and returned to the respective Counties and Towns, to-wit, Guilford – Messrs. David Caldwell, Joseph Hinds, Ralph Gorrell, Charles Bruce, ISHAM BROWDER. . .”

In said capacity, Isham Browder must have served on several committees, but nothing is in the records for that. However, according to the records, he did serve as a witness during a hearing on November 20, 1776. In said capacity, Isham Browder also voted on a bill on December 7, 1776, according to the same records (Saunders, William L.  Colonial Records of North Carolina, Vol. X, 1775-1776, (pp. 58; 913-914 and 960 “The Journal of the Provincial Congress of North Carolina, Held at Halifax, November the Twelfth Day, Anno. Dom. 1776”).

Isham Browder is found in the Rowan County, North Carolina Court Minutes as early as July 21, 1768, he at that time being appointed as an overseer of the road from the Orange County line on the north side of the Dan River, crossing the lower Sorow [Saura] Town Ford, and from there to John Walker’s. 

There is evidence that Isham Browder served as a Captain in the Guilford County militia during the American Revolutionary War.  This evidence first comes from a paper entitled “Colonel Absolom Bostick” by Harry Z. Tucker, written in 1938.  In it, Tucker writes:

“Don Ferdinand Bostick [a son of Col. Absolom Bostick], who married Elizabeth Rand of Halifax County Virginia died in 1824 and left several children.  Only one is known, William Rand Bostick [1803-after 1870], who married Miss Browder, a descendant of CAPTAIN ISHAM BROWDER, a soldier of the Revolution.”

The “Miss Browder” referred to in this paper was named Jane. Now, we know that Jane Browder was NOT a descendant of Isham Browder, since court records show that she was a daughter of Thompson Browder of Dinwiddie County, Virginia. But, that does not negate the possibility that Isham Browder might have been a Captain in some capacity during the Revolutionary War. In fact, Isham Browder’s initial property claim in the area can here be presented, which shows that he was a Captain in the local militia. It was entered on January 4, 1779, and was granted on October 22, 1782. Note that the claim was for 640 acres, but that, once the survey was done, it turned out to be 569 acres (Guilford County Land Grant Patent Book 48, p. 63; Entry #155; File #479; Grant #470): 

State of North Carolina                                           No. 755

Ralph Gorrell Entry officer for land in the County of Guilford

To the surveyor of the said County Greeting

You are hereby required as soon as may be to lay off and survey for CAPT. ISHAM BROWDER a tract or Parcel of land containing six hundred and forty Acres laying (sic.) in the county aforesaid where Lawrence Bankston’s line comes to the Creek thence along William Garrett’s line South to the corner thence a south course (through?) the iron works road thence East to John Smith’s Entry thence North to John Browder’s claim for Compliment.

Observing the Directions of the act of assembly in such case made and Provided for running out lands Two (sic.) Test and … Plan of Such survey with a Proper certificate annexed to Each you are to … with this Warrant to the Secretary’s office without Delay.

Given under my Hand at Springfield the fourth day of January Ammo Dom. 1779

                                                                                Ralph Gorrell

Surveyed 14th March 1780 for ISHAM BROWDER five Hundred and Sixty nine acres of land answering to the annexed Plan Situate on the South side Wolf Island Creek In the County of Guilford beginning at a box Elder John Browder’s Corner thence South on William Garrett’s line fifty six poles to a white Oak Thence East on said line Seventy four poles to a black Oak thence South on said One Hundred and Eighty poles to a black Oak on Spiar’s line thence East on said line Two hundred and Sixty Eight poles to a black Oak on John Smith’s line thence North on said line Two hundred and Eight poles to a Post Oak thence East on said line One hundred and thirty six poles to a Red Oak thence North on Smith’s line One hundred and six poles to a Red Oak thence South Eighty Degrees  West four hundred and seventy six poles to the first station.

David Loyd                                                             By Me

                                                                                   Abraham Philips D.S.

Isham Tatum

If, somehow, the reader did not notice – this document refers to Isham Browder as “Captain”.  There can be no mistake here.  So, there can no longer be any doubt that this man, Isham Browder, was indeed a Captain in the local Guilford County militia during the Revolutionary War, just as his friend, Abraham Philips (who surveyed the above), also was.  Perhaps Tucker had seen this document; something I had not seen in all of these years previous. But Tucker obviously assumed that Jane was a son of Isham because he did not have enough information.

It must here be noted that these land survey documents from the state are not archived in inventory books like the deeds are.  Therefore, there is no reference source that can be given for this survey order other than copies are at the State Archives.  It should also be noted that the militia records for Guilford and Rockingham counties are missing, so they can no longer be consulted. 

But, if that isn’t enough, I ordered copies of this and other land documents from the North Carolina State Archives. The preceding document, I found, still is contained within a sleeve which clearly show “Capt Isham Browder”. Thus, there can no longer be any question that he served as a Captain during the Revolutionary War. A copy of said sleeve follows.

There can be no mistake here.  Tucker must have seen this document and, from that, extrapolated that Jane Browder was the descendant of this Capt. Isham Browder.  In effect, Tucker assumed that Isham Browder was her ancestor, but he had no real evidence for that.  Tucker just made a mistake, that’s all. 

It seems likely that Isham Browder must have taken some part in the Battle of Alamance (War of the Regulation) – May 16, 1771. He could easily also have taken part in the Moore’s Creek Bridge Campaign (occupation of Cross Creek) – Feb. 27, 1776; the Yadkin River Valley Campaign – 1779, the Battle of Shallow Ford – Oct. 14, 1780, and the Wilmington Campaign (skirmish at Moore’s Plantation) – 1781. And he certainly took part in the Rutherford Expedition of 1776.

And he is very likely to have taken part in the Battle of Cowpens, South Carolina – Jan. 17, 1781, and the Battle of Guilford Courthouse – March 15, 1781. The reasons for this are (1) the pension application for Isham Browder (1762-1830), who was from Virginia and who later moved to Kentucky, states that he had participated in these two latter battles. However, this Isham Browder was discharged from service in 1779, after being wounded at the Battle of Monmouth. There is no indication in any record that he ever returned to service in any capacity. Obviously, these two battles took place after he was discharged. So, it stands to reason that a simple error was made by Isham’s (of Kentucky) son, John, who made this statement. He had apparently learned of an Isham Browder who had participated in said battles and assumed that this was his father. And (2) Isham Browder of Guilford County, North Carolina resided all too close to Guilford Courthouse to have not participated at least in that battle since service was compulsory if one was physically able. He was also well-acquainted with both Abraham Philips (who was a Captain in the local militia) and William Bethell (who was a General in same), and he resided close to William Bethell’s muster ground.

This Isham Browder, by the way, is NOT the father of one Parthenia “Theney” Browder-Bailey as is falsely shown to be the case on ancestry.com. In fact, there was no such Parthenia Browder at all.

Posted byjmarkbrowderDecember 2, 2019January 8, 2025Posted inUncategorized3 Comments on Captain Isham Browder

The Mystery of Edmund Browder’s Wife’s Maiden Name Solved at Long Last

The Mystery of Edmund Browder’s Wife’s Maiden Name Solved at Long Last

By: John Mark Browder, B.A., M.S., M.T.S., M.Div. (updated Jan. 1, 2025)

Knowing that there had to be some unknown connection between the Bolling and several related families to Edmund and Elizabeth Browder, I have conducted extensive research into those family lines. My determination, based mainly on a document in which Elizabeth relinquished her dower rights to a certain parcel of land, is that Elizabeth was a daughter of Joseph Parsons (c1617-1654) who, according to the records, had more than one child by a previous wife before he married Jane Mosby (1624-p. 1686), widow of Thomas Gregory (c1620-1653); Jane later marrying Maj. John Stith (1625-1694). The fact of the matter is that all of the witnesses to her relinquishment of dower rights, as well as Thomas Gregory, whom the land was later sold to, trace directly back to Jane in some way.

I should begin by stating that my previous assumption that Elizabeth could have been an unknown daughter of Col. Robert Bolling and Jane Stith (daughter of John Stith and Jane Mosby) was in error. Frankly, I did express that the connection was tenuous at best, but still felt that it was the most likely connection based on the evidence I had at the time. That would still be true except for the fact that I have found more evidence now, having reevaluated the entire scenario, carefully going through many records to draw a new conclusion.

In any case, Col. Robert Bolling (December 26, 1646, All Hallows, Barking Parish, London, England-July 17, 1709, Kippax, Charles City County, Virginia) arrived in Virginia on October 2, 1660. He first married (1) Jane Rolfe (October 10, 1650, Henrico County, Virginia-1676, Kippax, Virginia). He later married (2) Anne Stith, daughter of Maj. John Stith (c1625-1692) and Jane “Gregory” (c1624-aft. 1686). My thought was that, even though others stated that he and Jane were married in 1681, I found that questionable because several years had elapsed since his first wife died. Although marriageable women were certainly in short supply, I could not fathom him trying to bring up his child without the help of a wife for so long a time-period. Frankly, this still doesn’t make sense to me, but it is now inconsequential after all because I now understand that he is not my ancestor after all.

In addition to this, the understanding that he and Anne were known to have had at least six unknown daughters, one of whom almost had to have been named Elizabeth, gave the scenario some plausibility. But if this were the case, Elizabeth would have had to have been their very first daughter in order to fit. Regardless, as will be shown, he and Anne were still relatives in any case.

Col Robert Bolling and his first wife, Jane Rolfe (descendant of Pocahontas) had one child only, Col. John Bolling I. He married Mary V. Kennon (June 29, 1679, Conjurer’s Neck, Henrico County, Virginia-June 29, 1727, Cobbs, Virginia), daughter of Dr. Richard Kennon of Conjuror’s Neck (also a member of the House of Burgesses) and Elizabeth Worsham on December 29, 1697 in Henrico County, Virginia.

Conversely, Col. Robert Bolling and Anne Stith had several children, including (again) at least six unknown and two known daughters.

In any case, we move on to John Stith, Sr. (c1625-1692), who was born at Kirkham, Lancastershire, England and died in Westover Parish, Charles City County, Virginia. He married Jane Mosby (c1624-p. 1686), widow of Thomas Gregory (c1620-1653) in December of 1656 in Charles City County, Virginia. Not all family historians seem to note that Jane had also been the widow of Joseph Parsons, who died not long after their marriage, she marrying John Stith in 1656.

Anyway, the will of John Stith was written on November 13, 1690 and was proven on April 3, 1694 in Charles City County, Virginia. The known children of Maj. John Stith and Jane Mosby were John (c1658-bef. 1724), Anne (c1659-1709), Jane (d. bef. 1709), Agnes (b. c1659) and Drury (c1670-January 1741).

John Stith, Jr., their son, married Mary Randolph (b. November 1663), daughter of Col. William Randolph and Mary Isham, in 1685. Their known children were Mary Randolph (b. c1711), William (c1707-September 19, 1755), and John (c1670-1758).

Drury Stith, Sr., son of Maj. John Stith and Jane Mosby, married Susanna Bathurst (c1674-aft. 1745) about 1694. The known children of Drury and Susanna Stith were Drury (c1695-June 1740), William (b. c1697), Jane Drury (b. c1699), Rebecca (b. c1695), and Mary Randolph (c1701-August 17, 1801).

Mary Randolph Stith, daughter of Drury and Susanna Stith, married Buller Herbert (b. c1693), son of John Herbert (c1664-March 17, 1704) and Frances Anderson (1665-1726/m. 1687 [daughter of Reynard Anderson (1640-1690) and Elizabeth Skiffen (1640-1694)]). The known children of Buller and Mary R. Herbert were John (b. April 4, 1724), Ann (b. March 21, 1726/27), Mary (1726/28, London-August 17, 1801), and Martha.

Mary Herbert, daughter of Buller and Mary R. Herbert, married Col. Augustine Claiborne. Interestingly, a list of Taxable Personal Property for the residents of Dinwiddie County, Virginia, 1782-1787, lists, among others, the following persons: Arthur Browder (one slave), Mrs. Mary Bolling, Robert Bolling, Jr., Jesse Browder (one slave), Richard Browder (six slaves), Thompson Browder (one slave), Augustine Claiborne, Jr., Col. Augustine Claiborne, and Buller Claiborne. So, they all resided relatively close to one another.

Col. Augustine Claiborne was born on January 14, 1720/21 at “Sweet Hall”, King William County, Virginia and died on May 3, 1787 at “Windsor”, Sussex County, Virginia. He was a son of Capt. Thomas Claiborne (1680-1732) and Anne Fox (1684-1733) and, therefore, a brother of Nathaniel Claiborne. Augustine Claiborne is listed in the DAR Patriot Index along with his sons Buller, Richard and John Herbert.

The known children of Augustine and Mary Claiborne were Susanne (b. November 29, 1751), Mary Herbert (January 18, 1744/45-July 25, 1775), Herbert (b. April 7, 1746), Augustine (b. February 2, 1747/48), Anne (b. December 30, 1749), Buller (b. October 27, 1755), William (b. November 2, 1754), Elizabeth (1756-1794), Richard (b. 1757), John Herbert (b. May 30, 1763), Lucy Herbert (August 22, 1760-1823), and Bathurst (b. April 6, 1774).

Now, Anne Stith, daughter of John Stith and Jane Mosby, was born in Charles City County, Virginia and died at Kippax, Charles City County, Virginia. Again, she married Col. Robert Bolling at Kippax, Culpepper County, Virginia.

The known children of Col. Robert and Anne Bolling were Robert “Robin” (January 25, 1681-1749), Stith (b. March 28, 1686), Capt. Edward “Ned” (October 1, 1687-1710), Anne (b. July 22, 1690), Drury (June 21, 1695-1726), Thomas (b. March 20, 1696), Agnes (November 30, 1700-1762), and Mary “Molly” (b. 1702).

Anne Bolling, daughter of Col. Robert and Anne Bolling, married Robert Wynne, son of Capt. Thomas Wynne and Agnes Stith. Drury Bolling, son of Col. Robert and Anne Bolling, married Elizabeth Meriwether. Agnes Bolling, daughter of Col. Robert and Anne Bolling, married Richard Kennon (December 5, 1684-March 8, 1688).  Mary Bolling, daughter of Col. Robert and Anne Bolling, married Andrew Baker.

Finally, Elizabeth Bolling, daughter of Robert “Robin” Bolling and Anne Cocke, married James Mumford (d. c1754) on January 27, 1727). Their children were Martha (b. September 28, 1728), James (b. September 16, 1732), Susanna (March 29, 1734-October 1810), William, Robert, Thomas, and Edward (b. c1725).

James Mumford was born in Charles City (later Prince George) County, Virginia and he died before   September 15, 1690 at same. James Mumford and his wife, Sarah Wallace, were the parents of Maj. Robert  Mumford I, who died before November 13, 1739 in Prince George (later Dinwiddie) County, Virginia.

His son, Capt. Robert Mumford II, married Anna Bland (b. February 25, 1710/11), daughter of Richard Bland, Sr. (b. August 11, 1665, Berkeley, Charles City County, Virginia) and Elizabeth Randolph (b. 1680). Robert Mumford II was born in Prince George County, Virginia and died after September 8, 1740 (about 1744) at same.  Their children were Robert III, Elizabeth and Theodorick.

After the death of Robert Mumford II about 1744, Anna remarried to (2) George Currie. Robert Mumford III was a Lunenburg County, Virginia Court Justice in 1763 and 1764 (Bell, The Old Free State, p. 331).

Maj. Robert Mumford I had two other sons, James and William. James Mumford married Elizabeth Bolling, daughter of Robert “Robin” Bolling II and Anne Cocke, in 1727/28 in Prince George County. Robert Bolling II, again, was the son of Col. Robert Bolling I and Anne Stith.

It’s clear as mud, right? How would anyone sift through all of this and figure out how Elizabeth might be related to them? That question brings me back to the document in question.

Edmund Browder I purchased land, located on Gravelly Run in Prince George County, which was surveyed on November 6, 1719 (Prince George County, Virginia Deed Book III, p. 750). Elizabeth Browder, his wife, also signed a paper relinquishing her dower rights (the part or interest of a deceased man’s real estate allotted by law to his widow for her lifetime) in a certain parcel of land which was also located in Prince George County, in the year 1720/21 (PGCW&D 1713-28, p. 482). This was the same land that Edmund had surveyed in 1719. Edmund W. Browder, their son, was a witness to this transaction, along with Maj. Robert Mumford I (who married Martha “Patsy” Kennon), Buller Herbert (who married Mary R. Stith), and Col. John Bolling I (descendant of Pocahontas). Elizabeth’s attorney for this transaction was Lewis Green, presumed half-brother of Burwell Green.

Edmund Browder I sold this land within the same year to Thomas Gregory on February 13, 1720. Edmund also leased 50 acres of land on the south side of Blackwater Swamp to Thomas Gregory on August 7, 1721 (PGCDB III, p. 482).

Here, one must remember that Anne Stith’s mother was Jane Mosby, widow of Thomas Gregory. However, it is obvious that the two Thomas Gregorys cannot be one and the same. So, the question arises as to exactly who this Thomas Gregory is. The logical conclusion is that he was likely the son of Jane’s deceased husband and, in fact, that is the case, for he is known to have had at least one son, whose name was Thomas according to the records. In October of 1656 Charles City County Court testimony states that Joseph Parsons during his lifetime appointed one roan mare to the used of Thomas Gregory, son of Thomas Gregory, decd. (Southern Families XII, pp. 10-54).

Anyway, again, the witnesses to Elizabeth’s relinquishment of dower rights were all related, in some way connected to Jane Mosby. Or, more accurately, they and/or their wives were. First, Col. John Bolling, again, married Mary Kennon in 1697. She was the daughter of Dr. Richard Kennon and Elizabeth Worsham. Richard’s father was John Kennon who married Elizabeth Bolling (c1625-c1705). But his real connection is through his step-mother, Anne Stith, who was the daughter of Maj. John Stith and Jane Mosby. Second, Maj. Robert Mumford I married Martha Kennon, daughter of Dr. Richard Kennon and Elizabeth Worsham. Remember, his son, James, married another Elizabeth Bolling (daughter of Robert Bolling II [son of Col. Robert Bolling and Anne Stith] and Anne Cocke) in 1727/28. So, both of these are connected via the Kennons and the Bollings, and to Jane Mosby via Anne Stith. Third, Buller Herbert married Mary R. Stith, daughter of Drury Stith, Sr. (son of Maj. John Stith and Jane Mosby) and Susanna Bathurst. All three lead directly to Jane Mosby, so she HAS to be the connection.

But that doesn’t really get us to where we need to be. The key, in fact, lies with Thomas Gregory, Jr. Now, I will never state that using logic and reason can always get the researcher to the correct conclusion but, in this case, it was certainly helpful in that it only stands to reason that the property in question would have been sold to whoever Elizabeth’s closest living relative was, as was often (but not always) the case. So, I searched all records I could find to see how Thomas might have been related to Elizabeth as well as these others, obviously focusing on Jane Mosby as important. Based on everything I was able to find, I sadly had to discount descent from Thomas Gregory himself. I simply could find reference to the possibility that he had any children other than Thomas, Jr., although there are some references to more than one Elizabeth Gregory as a daughter of a Thomas Gregory from English records – which Thomas Gregory cannot be ascertained. For the record, all of them reference births that took place after the known death year of this Thomas Gregory, Sr. in any case. So, Elizabeth’s maiden name was not Gregory.

That necessarily leads to the second best possibility, that Elizabeth could have been a daughter of Joseph Parsons. The truly sad thing is that Joseph Parsons died not very long after his marriage to Jane Mosby, which is probably why some researchers overlook him. He died in 1655/56, with Jane marrying another time in 1656 to Maj. John Stith.

Now, I quickly ascertained that when Joseph and Jane were married, Joseph was already the father of an infant. I also quickly found out that her name had been Judith (said by some to have possibly married Richard Mosby, son of Edward Mosby and sister of Jane Mosby). That might have been the end of my search if not for the following record. From Charles City County, Virginia Court order Book, 1658-1661, page 277:

1654, To the worpll Cort of Cha. Citty Coun Edward Mosby humbly presenteth that he hath necessarily paid out of the [e]state of Judith Parsons for her parte of sev’all debts recov’ed and all owed as per record appeareth the sum of 5,123 lb tobbo and caske as per ac ‘ct &c Wherefore he humbly craveth a discounpt and deduccon of so much out of her estate for s’ch he is obliged at the Same rate in money as the said estate is valued to him by Capt Perry & Capt Stegge and he shall pay &c.

June 3 1661, Rec July 15 1661 p 278. Abstract “Whereas Edwoard Mosby Guardian of Judith Parsons one of the orphanes of Joseph Parsons dec’d having pd from her estate several debts due from her father’s estate, amounting to 5123 lb tobo, order that this be allowed.”

ONE OF THE ORPHANS OF JOSEPH PARSONS, DECD. It is clear from this record that Judith Parsons and other orphans of Joseph Parsons were still living.

Further, “Dec. 11, 1663: “Itt is ord’ed that Judith the Orphand of Joseph Parsons dec’d be rendred and del’red into the care and tuicon of Lt John Stith who maried the mother in Law of the sd Orphand and that the Accct of her estate be presented at the next Com Co’rt.”

And from the estate of Judith Parsons:

“For tobbo pd Capt Stegge for her part of 1200 lb tob recov’ed for Mr Adenbrook 2666 for tobbo allowed John Stith for her part of Debts recorded there and in the Countrey as by record 1664.”

Further, I eventually also found reference to Joseph Parsons II, who lived at least to 1679 and who was closely associated with Thomas Gregory II. And both became Quakers. But I have not found a single reference to his estate or other interests in the court records. The only one mentioned is Judith Parsons. So, it should not be difficult to understand that Judith is mentioned because she died early whereas the other orphans lived to adulthood, however many there were.

It seems perfectly obvious to me now that Elizabeth, wife of Edmund Browder I, was a daughter of Joseph Parsons and his first wife. Her nearest living relative, since Judith had obviously passed away, would have been Thomas Gregory, Jr., a half-brother because his mother was Jane Mosby. Even though we do not know, from any records I have found thus far, that Joseph Parsons had a daughter named Elizabeth, there is really no good reason to discount this in my opinion.

The remaining question is just who Joseph’s first wife was. Sadly, I have found no record that suggests a name.

Select Bibliography:

Brock, R. A. and Wyndham Robertson.  Pocahontas, Alias Matoaka, And Her Descendants Through Her

Marriage at Jamestown, Virginia, in April, 1614, With John Rolfe, Gentleman, etc.  Baltimore:    Genealogical Publishing Co., Inc., 1982.

Brown, Stuart E. and Lorraine F. Meyers and Eileen M. Chappel.  Pocahontas’ Descendants. Baltimore: Genealogical Publishing Co., Inc., 1994.

Posted byjmarkbrowderSeptember 19, 2019February 7, 2025Posted inUncategorizedLeave a comment on The Mystery of Edmund Browder’s Wife’s Maiden Name Solved at Long Last

Our Isham and Related Family Heritage

Our Isham and Related Family Heritage

By: John Mark Browder, B.A., M.S., M.T.S., M.Div. (updated and corrected, Jan. 8, 2025)

The surname “Isham” comes from the Parish of Isham, Northamptonshire, England, near Kettering. It is pronounced “Eye-shum” and is derived from a brook called “Ise” which flows through the western part of England. The surname itself was, therefore, first found in Northamptonshire at Isham, a Saxon village and civil parish which dates back to 974 when it was listed as “Ysham”. By the Doomsday Book of 1086, the place name had evolved to the present spelling of Isham.

The place name literally means “homestead by the River Ise,” “ice home”, or “settlement by water”, having derived from the Celtic river-name + the Old English “ham” or “hamm”. The earliest record of the family was found here “where an elder branch of the existing family, Isham of Lamport, were seated soon after the [Norman] Conquest” of 1066.

It is understood, though perhaps not quite proven, that the Isham family line begins with a guy named simply Azo. Azo was born about 1060 and died about 1120 in Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England. He had a son named Roger who adopted as his surname, “de Isham“. Roger de Isham was born about 1100 and died about 1160 at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England. Roger de Isham had a son, Henry de Isham, who was born about 1140, presumably at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England, he also dying there in about 1200. Henry de Isham had a son, Thomas de Isham (c1180-c1250), born and died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England. Thomas de Isham had a son, Robert de Isham (c1220-c1280), born and died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England. Robert de Isham had a son, Henry de Isham (c1260-c1330), born and died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England.

Henry de Isham is the first in this line of whom we know a wife’s first name. She was Agnes (d. p. 1349). She also died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England. Henry de Isham and Agnes had a son, Robert de Isham (c1300-c1360), born and died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England. Robert de Isham had a son, Robert de Isham (c1335-1402), born and died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England.

Robert de Isham married Juliane (d. p. 1376), who also died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England. Robert de Isham and Juliane had a son, Robert de Isham (1377-March 13, 1423), born and died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England.

Robert de Isham had a son, Robert Isham I (1402-1475), born and died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England. Notice that he dropped the “de” from his ancestral name. He married Elizabeth Knostone (b. Irchester, Northamptonshire, England), daughter of Aston Knostone (b. 1373).  Robert and Elizabeth also died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England. 

The known children of Robert Isham and Elizabeth Knostone were William (1427-June 13, 1516), Robert II, Richard, and John. 

William Isham, son of Robert Isham and Elizabeth Knostone, married Elizabeth Brampspeth (1427-Sept. 20, 1478), daughter of Thomas Brampspeth (b. 1401).  Elizabeth was the widow of Robert Braunspeth.

William Isham was born and died at 1427 at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England.  Elizabeth Brampspeth was also born at Glooston, Harborough District, Leicestershire, England.  She died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England. 

The known children of William Isham and Elizabeth Brampspeth were Thomas (1456-1547), William, and John, all born at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England.

Sir Lord Thomas Isham, son of William Isham and Elizabeth Brampspsth, married Lady Ellen de Vere (c1456-c1547), daughter of Sir Richard de Vere (b. c1430, Addington, Northamptonshire, England) and Dame Isabella de Greene (b. 1434).  Isabella was a daughter of Walter de Greene and granddaughter of John de Greene and Margaret Greene.

Sir Lord Thomas Isham was born in 1456 at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England and died in 1547 at same.  The known children of Thomas Isham and Ellen de Vere were Henry, Eusaby and John. 

A digression into the ancestral heritage of the de Vere and related families follows.  I do not claim this to be completely accurate, but it is believed to be by other researchers. 

Alberic II held the title of Baron de Vere.  He married Lady Adeliza de Clare, daughter of Gilbert de Tousburg (second Earl of Clare) and Lady Adeliza (daughter of Hugh de Clermont, Count of Beauvois, and Lady Margaret, daughter of Heldwin IV, Count de Rouci, and Lady Adela).  Heldwin was the son of Elbo I, Count de Rouci, and Lady Beatrix, daughter of Rynerius XI, Count of Haniault, and Princess Hedwige.  Princess Hedwige was the daughter of Hugh Capet, King of France, and Adela, daughter of William, Duke of Aquitaine, and Adelheid.

Adelheid was the daughter of Otto I, The Great (son of Henry I, King of Saxony “The Fowler”), and Adelheid.  Otto was born in 912 and died in 973.  Adelheid, his daughter, was born in 951. 

Otto was crowned Emperor of the Holy Roman Empire in 936.  In 951 he crossed the Alps on a military campaign and declared himself to also be King of Italy.  He then married Adelheid, widow of Lothary (who had previously been King of Italy).  However, Otto was forced to return to Germany following his brief military campaign because, at that time, a series of revolts were taking place in Germany, as well as an invasion by the Magyars.  The Magyars were later crushed by Otto’s armies in the Battle of the Lech River in 955.  In 961 he crossed the Alps again in order to put down an uprising in Roma, Italy.

The son of Alberic II and Adeliza, Sir Robert de Vere, married Dame Matilda de Furnel, daughter of Robert de Furnel.  Their son, Sir Henry de Vere, married Lady (Dame) Hildeburga.  The son of Sir Henry de Vere and Lady Hildeburga, Sir Walter de Vere, married Dame Lucia Bassett.

The son of Sir Walter de Vere and Dame Lucia Bassett, Sir Henry de Drayton, married Dame Ivetta Bourdon, daughter of Sir William Bourdon.  Sir Henry de Drayton adopted the name “Manor.”  Sir Henry and Dame Ivetta had a son named Baldwin.  Sir Baldwin de Drayton married Dame Idonee Ginueges.  They in turn had a son, Sir John de Drayton who married Dame Phillipa d’Ardene.  And their daughter was Katherine Drayton.

Roger de La Zouche had a son, Lord Allan de La Zouche (d. 1269) who married Lady Eline de Quincy (1202-August 20, 1296), daughter of Roger de Quincy (1174, Winchester, Hampshire, England-April 25, 1264) and Helen de MacDonal of Galloway (m. 1202, Brackley, Aberdeenshire, Scotland), and grand-daughter of Lord Saier de Quincy II (b. 1150) and Lady Margaret de Bellmont (or Beaumont), daughter of Robert de Beaumont III (c1130, Leichester, Leichestershire, England-August 31, 1190, Duras, Greece), 3rd Earl of Liecester, and Petronilla Grentesmaisnill, (m. c1155).  Saier de Quincy II, son of Saier de Quincy I, was 1st Earl of Winchester.  Roger de Quincy was 2nd Earl of Winchester.  Helen de MacDonal was the daughter of Lord Alan of Galloway, son of Roland of Galloway and Helena of Morville, and unknown de Lacy, daughter of Roger de Lacy and Maud de Clare.  Roger de Quincy and Helen de MacDonal had another daughter named Margaret de Quincy who married William Ferrers and had a daughter named Joan who married Thomas Berkeley in 1267 and they in turn had a son named Sir Maurice Berkeley.

Lord Allan and Lady Eline de la Zouche had a son, Lord Eudo de La Zouche, who married Lady Millicent de Canteloupe.  They had a daughter, Lucy de La Zouche, who married Thomas de Greene II, son of Thomas de Greene I (b. 1292).  Thomas II and Lucy had a son, Sir Henry de Greene I (b. 1369), who married Dame Katherine de Drayton, daughter of John de Drayden.  Henry and Catherine had a son, Sir Henry de Greene II, who married Dame Matilda de Mandel, daughter of Sir Thomas Mandel.  Henry and Matilda had a son, John de Greene, who married Margaret Green, daughter of John de Greene.  John and Margaret de Greene had a son, Walter de Greene and a daughter, Isabella de Greene.

John de Drayden was the son of Baldwin de Drayden and Idonee Ginueges.  Baldwin de Drayden was the son of Sir Henry de Drayden.  Idonee Ginueges was the daughter of Ivetta Bourdon.  Sir Henry de Drayden was the son of Sir Walter de Vere (1304-November 5, 1387) and Dame Lucia Bassett (b. 1308), daughter of Gilbert Bassett (b. 1282).

Sir Walter de Vere was the son of Lord Henry de Vere (son of Robert de Vere) and Lady Hildaburga.  Lady Hildaburga’s mother was Matilda, daughter of Robert de Furnel.

Henry Isham, son of Sir Lord Thomas Isham and Lady Ellen de Vere, married Johan Busley, daughter of Robert Catalyne.  Henry Isham and Johan Busley had a son named Eusebius “Euseby” Isham (1486-1546).  This family also resided at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England where Henry was born and where they all died.

Lord Eusebius “Euseby” Isham, son of Henry Isham and Johan Busley, married Dame Anne Pulton (1495-Dec. 11, 1546), daughter of Sir Lord Giles Pulton (1460-1548) and Katherine Lovett (1464-1548).  Lord Euseby Isham is buried at All Saints Church, Pytchley, Northamptonshire, England.  Anne also died at Pytchley and is presumably buried at the same church. 

The known children of Lord Euseby Isham and Dame Anne Pulton were Giles (d. Aug. 31, 1559), Robert (d. 1564), Gregory (c1520-Sept. 4, 1558), John (c1525-Mar. 17, 1595/96), Henry (b. c1528), Katharina, Ellen, Elizabeth, Edith, and Isabel.

Lord Gregory Isham married Lady Elizabeth Dale on Dec. 16, 1548, at Saint Pancras Soper Lane, London, England.

Sir Eusebius “Euseby” Isham (Feb. 26, 1552-June 11, 1626), son of Lord Gregory Isham and Lady Elizabeth Dale, married Dame Anne Borlase (d. Dec. 1627), daughter of Sir John Borlase Sheriff of Buckinghamshire, Esq. (1526-May 6, 1593) and Dame Anne Lytton.  Sir Euseby Isham was born and died at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England.  He and Dame Anne resided at Braunston, Northamptonshire, England and later moved back to Pytchley.  Sir Eusaby is buried at Pytchley Church in Pytchley. 

The known children of Sir Euseby Isham and Dame Anne Borlase were Anne, John (d. Dec. 11, 1626), Mary, Euseby, William (Mar. 15, 1587-June 11, 1626), Sarah (d. June 10, 1627), Anthony (d. Jan. 26, 1603/04), Anne, Thomas (1600-Feb. 6, 1669/70), Susan, and Elizabeth (d. 1623). 

William Isham married Mary Brett (1603-1675), daughter of William Brett (d. 1624), at Toddington, Bedfordshire, England on August 15, 1625.  William was born at Braunston, Northamptonshire, England.  Mary was born at Hearne, Toddington, Bedfordshire, England.  Their known children were Euseby (1626-January 30, 1653/54), and Capt. Henry I (1628-December 1, 1676). 

Alexander Brett was born in 1504 at Whitstaton, Devonshire, England.  He married Rosemaderos, who was born in 1507 at Whitstaton, Devonshire, England.  They had at least one son, Robert Brett I, who was born in 1531 in England.  He married Elizabeth Bush, daughter of Edward Bush (b. c1508, England).  Elizabeth was born in 1534 in England.

Robert Brett I and Elizabeth Bush had a son, Robert Brett II, born about 1551 in London, County Middlesex, England.  He married Elizabeth Highgate, daughter of Reginald Highgate (b. c1529, London, England).  Elizabeth was born about 1555 in London, County Middlesex, England. 

Robert Brett II and Elizabeth Highgate had a son, William Brett (b. 1577 or 1583, Hearne, Toddington, Bedfordshire, England).  William’s wife was born about 1577, but her name is not known.  William died in 1624 in England. 

William Brett and wife had a daughter, Mary Brett, who married William Isham, as well as at least one son, Sir Edward Brett, who was knighted by King Charles I for his military excellence.  Sir Edward Brett left extensive property to the children of Henry Isham; Henry, Ann, and Mary.  

Capt. Henry Isham I, son of William Isham and Mary Brett, was born at Pytchley, Kettering Borough, Northamptonshire, England in 1628 and resided at Canterbury, County Kent, England.  He immigrated to Henrico County, Virginia about 1656, and built a plantation at Bermuda Hundred in Henrico County in 1661. 

It is said that Henry actually descended from a minor branch of the Isham family in England, which did not possess extensive land holdings there.  But once in the American Colonies, the family greatly prospered. 

Capt. Henry Isham I married Katherine Banks (c1627-1686), daughter of Christopher Banks (b. 1603, Canterbury, County Kent, England) on December 1, 1656 in Henrico County, Virginia.  Katherine was born at Canterbury, County Kent, England and later resided at Bermuda Hundred, Virginia.  She died at Amherst, Henrico County, Virginia.

Katherine had first married (1) Capt. Joseph Royall II (c1600-Mar. 10, 1655), marrying (2) Capt. Henry Isham I after Joseph’s death.  It happens that my line is descended from Katherine Banks through three family lines, the Isham family, the Jopling family, and the Eppes family. 

Capt. Henry Isham I became “one of tidewater Virginia’s largest landowners and the social arbiter of the families living on the south bank of the James River.”  Henry died on December 1, 1676 at Bermuda Hundred.   Katherine left a Will of her own dated August 10, 1686 in Henrico County, Virginia. 

The known children of Capt. Henry Isham and Katherine Banks were Henry II (d. 1678), Mary (1658/60-Dec. 29, 1735), and Anne Elizabeth (c1665, King and Queen County, Virginia-1718).  Note here that we are descended from both Mary and Anne.

Henry Isham II died at sea without having been married.  Anne Elizabeth Isham married Col. Francis “Frank” Eppes III (c1659-Jan. 1718).  Mary Isham was born at Bermuda Hundred, Henrico County, Virginia and died at Turkey Island in Henrico County.  She married Col. William Randolph I (Nov. 7, 1650-Apr. 11, 1711). 

The Will of Katherine Banks-Isham bequeathed to her grandson, William Randolph, £20, to her grandson, Henry Randolph £5, to her granddaughters Mary Randolph and Elizabeth Randolph, each “all such money that is now in the hands of my son-in-law [Col.] William Randolph”; the residue of money in the hands of her son-in-law, [Col.] William Randolph, to go to her daughters Mary Randolph (wife of Col. William Randolph) and Ann Eppes (wife of Col Francis Epes), equally; the above two daughters to be divided that which was on her trunk, with each to have two silver salt cellars. 

Her daughter, Mary Randolph, was bequeathed Katherine’s wedding ring, best feather bed, best silver tankard, and 15 shillings to buy a mourning ring. 

Her grandson, Joseph “Royal” (Capt. Joseph Royall III), received one servant named John Townes, for the time he had left to serve, and Katherine’s small silver tankard. 

Also, to “every child of my son Royal” (Capt. Joseph Royall III) were bequeathed two silver spoons.  Also, to her “loving son Joseph Royal, my best tankard”; to her grandson Richard Denis, one of her best cows and 2 silver spoons; to her grandson Isham Eppes (son of Col. Francis Eppes and Ann Elizabeth Isham), her “negro man Dick”; to her grandson Francis Eppes (son of same), her “best silver tankard; but one” to the child of her daughter, Ann Eppes, her “largest silver porringer and great cup”; her sealed ring, great hoop ring, pair of silver clasps, and silver bodkin were to go to her daughter, Anne Eppes. 

To her grandson, Richard Perrin (son of Richard Perrin and Katherine Royall), 1 feather bed and furniture; to her granddaughter, Sarah Royal, 1 yearling heifer; to her granddaughters, Katherine Farrar, Mary, Sarah, and Anne Perrin, each 2 silver spoons; to Katherine Farrar 1 Guinea; to Anne Perrin, one silver porringer; to her granddaughter, Sarah Dennis, 2 silver spoons and 1 pair of dowlass sheets;

To her two daughters, Sarah Wilkinson and Katherine Perrin, all her wearing clothes; to her “loving friend Mary Parker, 6 ells of best dowlas and as much of the finest serge to make a gown and petticoat”; to her grandson, Maiden Marshall, 1 two-year old heifer; to her son, Joseph Royall, all her land;

All of her crop of corn was to go to her executors, except enough to buy 2 gravestones, “one to cover me and other my dear decd. husband”.  All the rest was to be divided between her four children: Sarah Wilkinson, Joseph Royal, Katherine Perrin, and Anne Eppes, and to each 15 shillings for mourning rings. 

Executors were her son, Joseph Royall, and son-in-law Francis Eppes.  She instructed that her body was to be buried “near my dead husband on my own plantation”. 

Witnesses were John Worsham, Nathaniel Hill, and Littlebury Eppes.  The Will was probated on December 1, 1686 (1677-1692 Will and Deed Book, p. 392-301). 

A further digression of the Isham and de Vere families follows.  Obviously, all of this cannot be proven, but it is provided here just the same.

Lady Margaret de Bellmont was the great-granddaughter of Robert Beaumont II (1104, Leicester, Leicestershire, England-April 5, 1168, England) and Amice de Gael (m. after 1120).  Robert Beaumont II was the son of Robert Beaumont I and Isabel Vermandois.  Isabel was the daughter of Hugh Magnus and Adelhart de Vermandois.  Adelhart was the daughter of Herbert de Vermandois IV and Adele de Vexin.  Herbert IV was the son of Otho Vermandois (d. 1045) and Pavie.  Otho was the son of Herbert Vermandois III and Ermengarde.  Herbert III was the son of Albert Vermandois I (c917, Vermandois, Normandy, France-September 8, 987, St. Quentin, France-buried at Pas-de-Calais, France/m. before 954) and Gerberga (m. France).  Albert I was the son of Herbert Vermandois II (c884, Vermandois, Normandy, France-February 23, 942, St. Quentin, France) and Hildebrante.

Herbert II was the son of Count Herbert Vermandois I (c848, Vermandois, Normandy, France-948) and Countess Berthe Vermandois.  Count Herbert was the son of Pepin Quentin Vermandois II.  Pepin was the son of Berhard (d. 812) and Cunegonde.  Berhard was the son of Pepin, King of Lombardy (April 773-July 8, 810) (brother of Emperor Louis I the Pious of the Franks/ m. Judith of Bavaria) and Bertha de Toulouse.  Pepin I was the son of Charlemagne, King of France (b. April 2, 742, Aix-la-Chappelle, France/m. c771) and Hildegarde de Suabia of Alemmania, daughter of Rudapert.  Charlemagne was the son of King Pepin III “The Short” of the Franks (714, Lombardy, France-767, Milan, Italy) and Bertha de Laon II.  Pepin, King of Lombardy, France was the son of Charles Martel, King of France (689-741) and Rotrude.

Emperor Louis I the Pious of the Franks and Judith of Bavaria, daughter of Count Welf of Alemmania and Heilwig, had a son, Charles the Bald.  Charles married Ermentrude D’Orleans, daughter of Earl Vodon of Orleans and Engeltrude, and they had a daughter, Judith of the Franks, who married (1) King Ethelwulf of the Wessex and, later, (2) Count Baldwin I of Flanders in 862.  Judith and King Ethelwulf had a son named King Alfred the Great of England.  Judith and Baldwin had a son named Count Baldwin II of Flanders.                 Charles Martel was the son of Pepin de Mayor and Alpais.  Pepin was the son of Duke Ansgise (d. 685/m. before 639) and Begga.  Ansgise was the son of Saint Arnulf (b. about August 13, 582) and Dode “Clothilde”.  Arnulf was the son of Arnoul, Bishop of Metz and Oda de Savoy.  Arnoul was the son of Ausbert (b. 570) and Blithildes.  Ausbert was the son of Ferreolus, grand-son of Sigermerus I.  Sigermerus I was the son of King Clodius the Long-Haired (b. Westphalia, d. 455) and Basina de Thuringina.  Clodius was the son of King Pharamond of the Franks (c370-430) and Argotta.  Pharamond was the son of King Marcomir of the East Franks (c356-404).  Marcomir was the son of King Clodius I of the Franks.  Clodius was the son of King Dagobert II (303-379).  Dagobert was the son of Duke Genebald I of the East Franks.  Genebald was the son of King Dagobert I (d. 317).  Dagobert was the son of King Walter (d. 306).  Walter was the son of King Clodius III (225-298).  Clodius III was the son of King Bartherus.  Bartherus was the son of King Helderic (c185-253).  Helderic was the son of King Sunno (d. 213).  Sunno was the son of King Farrobert (d. 186).  Farrobert was the son of King Clodomir IV of the Franks (d. c186) and Hasilda.  Clodomir was the son of King Marcomir IV of Franconi (before 105-c149) and Athildis.  Marcomir was the son of King Odomir of the Franks (c85-c128).  Odomir was the son of King Richemer I of the Franks (c65-c90).  Richemer was the son of Ratherius II who was the son of Ratherius I who was the son of Antenor IV, son of Clodomir III (c3-c50), son of Marcomir III (c17 BCE-c 50 CE), son of Clodius II (c 37 BCE-c 20 CE), son of Francus.
